[R11] Metformin, age-related cognitive decline, and brain pathology
(https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0197458023002427)
"""
AbstractThe objective of this study was to evaluate the relation of metformin with change in cognition and brain pathology. During a mean of 8 years (SD = 5.5) of annual follow-up visits, 262/3029 participants were using metformin at any time during the study. Using a linear-mixed effect model adjusted for age, sex, and education, metformin users had slower decline on a score of global cognition compared to non-users (estimate = 0.017, SE = 0.007, p = 0.027). Analyses of cognitive domains showed a slower decline in episodic memory and semantic memory specifically. In sensitivity analysis, when examining any diabetes medication use vs none, no association was observed of any diabetes medication use with cognitive function. In the autopsy subset of 1584 participants, there was no difference in the level of Alzheimer’s disease (AD) pathology or the presence of infarcts (of any size or location) between groups of metformin users vs non-users. However, in additional analyses, metformin users had higher odds of subcortical infarcts, and lower odds of atherosclerosis and arteriosclerosis.IntroductionDiabetes mellitus (DM) is now a well-recognized risk factor for cognitive impairment and dementia (Cheng et al., 2014, Gudala et al., 2013), and a recent commission proposed that it be one of the targets for dementia prevention (Biessels, 2020). Metformin, one of the oldest oral medications to treat DM, is a first-line agent as monotherapy, (Qaseem et al., 2017) recommended for use along with diet and exercise. There have been numerous human studies (Farr et al., 2019; Guo et al., 2014; Hartman et al., 2019; Koenig et al., 2017; Koo et al., 2019; Li et al., 2019; Lin et al., 2018; Luchsinger et al., 2016; Moore et al., 2013; Ng et al., 2014; Porter et al., 2019; Samaras et al., 2020; Shi et al., 2019; Wennberg et al., 2018; Wu et al., 2020; Zhou et al., 2015; Zhu et al., 2020) evaluating the effect of metformin on cognition, but results from human studies have been mixed. These studies varied widely in design, including sample sizes, follow-up length, and participants' cognitive profiles. For example, while some studies were randomized controlled trials with a few participants followed over weeks (Guo et al., 2014; Hartman et al., 2019; Koenig et al., 2017; Lin et al., 2018; Luchsinger et al., 2016), others were prospective observational cohorts with thousands of participants followed longitudinally for years (Koo et al., 2019; Moore et al., 2013; Ng et al., 2014; Porter et al., 2019; Samaras et al., 2020; Shi et al., 2019; Wennberg et al., 2018; Wu et al., 2020; Zhou et al., 2015). Also, a few studies have gender inequalities, such as predominantly men (Shi et al., 2019) or only women (Hartman et al., 2019), thus not generalizable. Some had small sample sizes ranging from 20 to 83 participants (Guo et al., 2014; Koenig et al., 2017; Lin et al., 2018; Luchsinger et al., 2016; Zhou et al., 2015), and one study only had a 6-month follow-up, reducing the likelihood to observe changes in cognition (Hartman et al., 2019). Some studies assessed the cognitive status with comprehensive test batteries (Guo et al., 2014; Koenig et al., 2017; Luchsinger et al., 2016; Porter et al., 2019; Samaras et al., 2020; Wennberg et al., 2018; Wu et al., 2020; Zhou et al., 2015), whereas others only used screening tools such as mini-mental state exam (MMSE) (Koo et al., 2019; Moore et al., 2013; Ng et al., 2014; Shi et al., 2019), and yet others evaluated for a clinical diagnosis of dementia using International Statistical Classification of Diseases and Related Health Problems codes (Shi et al., 2019). The differences in results could also be attributed, in part, to the differences in the study population. For example, Samaras et al. (2020) enrolled participants with normal cognition, whereas the Ng et al. (2014) study had MCI at baseline. Several studies reported an improvement in cognitive function (Guo et al., 2014; Koenig et al., 2017; Lin et al., 2018; Luchsinger et al., 2016; Ng et al., 2014; Samaras et al., 2020; Shi et al., 2019; Wu et al., 2020; Zhou et al., 2015), while others reported worsening (Koo et al., 2019; Moore et al., 2013; Porter et al., 2019) or no effect (Hartman et al., 2019, Wennberg et al., 2018). Yet, we are not aware of any human study concurrently evaluating the relationship of metformin to both brain function (cognition) and pathology, including the 2 most common causes of dementia, namely Alzheimer’s disease (AD) and cerebrovascular pathologies.The objective of this study was to evaluate the relation of metformin with age-related cognitive decline and postmortem brain pathology, in older community-dwelling adults. More than 3000 participants were followed with yearly clinical assessments, including detailed neuropsychological testing, medication inspection, and health information. We examined the relation of metformin use with the level and change in cognitive function in older participants who were without dementia at baseline. Additionally, in a subset of more than 1500 participants who died during the study and came to the autopsy, we evaluated the association of metformin with neurodegenerative AD pathology, as well as with cerebrovascular disease pathologies (brain infarcts and cerebral vessel pathologies). In additional analyses, we further investigated the relation of the broader group of participants taking any diabetes medication vs non-users with cognitive outcomes, as well as examined separate cognitive domains, and the timing of the initiation of the medication (either before or at enrollment in the study).Access through your organizationCheck access to the full text by signing in through your organization.Access through your organizationSection snippetsParticipantsWe used data from participants enrolled in 1 of 2 longitudinal, community-based, prospective cohort studies of aging and dementia. These studies were designed to have essentially identical recruitment techniques and a large overlap of data collection at the item level following training by the same trainers. This allows the efficient combining of data from the cohorts, to increase statistical power and facilitate studies on risk and protective factors. Briefly, the Religious Orders Study (ROS) (Participant characteristicsTable 1 shows the characteristics of the participants (total group, and 2 subgroups of metformin users and non-users), as well as for the autopsy subgroup, with regards to demographics, clinical characteristics, and medication use. Participants in the total group of 3029 participants were on average 78.7 years at baseline (SD = 7.4), and about 72% were women. They were followed annually for about 8.1 years (SD = 5.7). Of these 3029, 262 (9%) participants received metformin during the study. The DiscussionAmong more than 3000 older women and men without known dementia at baseline, and an average of 8 years of annual follow-up, we found that participants using metformin during the study did not differ in cognitive function at baseline, but had a slower rate of decline in global cognition. This association did not change, even after controlling for severity of diabetes. Further, the relation of metformin to cognition was primarily observed as slower declines in episodic memory and semantic memory. Output ONLY a valid JSON array. Each element must have the following fields:
[
  {
    "claim_id": 1,
    "claim": "<the claim text>",
    "label": "<CLEAR | VAGUE | AMBIGUOUS | INCORRECT | OMITTED>",
    "confidence": <0.0-1.0, how confident you are in this label>,
    "matched_references": ["R1", "R2"],
    "evidence": "<key phrase(s) from the reference that support your judgment>",
    "reasoning": "<brief explanation>"
  },
  ...
]

Rules:
1. You MUST output exactly one entry per claim, in the same order as the claims above.
2. matched_references should list ALL reference IDs (R1, R2, etc.) that are relevant to the claim. Use an empty list [] for OMITTED.
3. evidence should quote or closely paraphrase the specific text from references. Use "No relevant content found" for OMITTED.
4. confidence reflects how clearly the references support your judgment (1.0 = unambiguous match/contradiction, 0.5 = borderline).
5. CRITICAL: When a claim contains numbers, dates, or attributes paired with specific entities, verify that each value is assigned to the CORRECT entity. If the claim assigns value A to entity X and value B to entity Y, but the reference assigns value A to entity Y and value B to entity X, that is INCORRECT — the values are swapped. Do not label a claim CLEAR just because the same numbers appear in the reference; check that they are attributed to the same things.
6. Only answer with the specified JSON array, no other text.
